People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… thanks. I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling that certain people around the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are… sort of keeping tabs on me. Like, which clients I meet with, where I show up.

I know that probably sounds a little strange, and I’m not trying to make it into a big dramatic thing. It’s just… invitations I get, topics on panels, even where people sit near me at events — it starts to feel like they’re sending little signals. I do tell myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go, and it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.

I know two months maybe isn’t that long, but it’s been enough that I haven’t been able to just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the beliefs you have about people keeping tabs on you as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can see how most people would probably find it unusual, or maybe hard to follow if I explained all of it.\n\nI’m not trying to sound paranoid or anything. It’s just that I’ve gotten pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly watching which clients I meet with, and then sending these little signals — like through invitations, panel topics, or even who ends up sitting near me at events. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation… but I haven’t really been able to let it go.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that these people are actually keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: I’m… fairly certain, I guess. I don’t want to say I know it like a fact, because I realize that probably sounds unreasonable, and I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence.\n\nBut honestly, a lot of the time I feel convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I meet with and then sort of signaling things through invitations, panel topics, or even who sits near me. I know that may not sound very solid from the outside, but it’s hard for me to let go of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. A little, yeah… I mean, I’m still working, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart.

I guess… yes, probably. I mean, I can see how if I explained it to someone else, they might say it sounds like I’m reading too much into normal things.

But to me, it doesn’t feel random. Like an invitation will come through, or a panel topic will be just close enough to something I’ve been dealing with, or I’ll notice who ends up sitting near me at an event, and it feels like there’s a message in it—like people in those arts and finance circles are sort of keeping track of who I’m meeting with.

I know that may sound unusual. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I don’t want to sound unreasonable, but… I have a hard time letting it go once I notice it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… thanks. I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling that certain people around the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are… sort of keeping tabs on me. Like, which clients I meet with, where I show up.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little strange, and I’m not trying to make it into a big dramatic thing. It’s just… invitations I get, topics on panels, even where people sit near me at events — it starts to feel like they’re sending little signals. I do tell myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go, and it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these feelings of being kept tabs on?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about two months, give or take. It wasn’t like one clear day it started, more that I began noticing patterns with client meetings and those little signals, and then I realized I’d been thinking about it pretty consistently. I know two months maybe isn’t that long, but it’s been enough that I haven’t been able to just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the beliefs you have about people keeping tabs on you as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can see how most people would probably find it unusual, or maybe hard to follow if I explained all of it.\n\nI’m not trying to sound paranoid or anything. It’s just that I’ve gotten pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly watching which clients I meet with, and then sending these little signals — like through invitations, panel topics, or even who ends up sitting near me at events. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation… but I haven’t really been able to let it go.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that these people are actually keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: I’m… fairly certain, I guess. I don’t want to say I know it like a fact, because I realize that probably sounds unreasonable, and I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence.\n\nBut honestly, a lot of the time I feel convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I meet with and then sort of signaling things through invitations, panel topics, or even who sits near me. I know that may not sound very solid from the outside, but it’s hard for me to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever question or doubt whether these people are actually keeping tabs on you, or do you feel completely sure about it?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes, yeah. I mean, I’m not completely sure in the way I’d be sure about something I could prove.\n\nI’ll tell myself it could just be coincidence — like maybe the invitations or panel topics or who sits near me at events don’t mean anything. And I know that’s probably what you’d expect me to consider. But then when it keeps lining up with certain clients I’ve met with, I end up feeling pretty convinced again that people in those arts and finance circles are tracking it somehow.\n\nSo… I guess I have doubts, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being kept tabs on affected your daily routine or decisions in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m still working, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I guess my practice has gotten smaller than it used to be. Not in a dramatic way—I’m not cutting people off or anything.\n\nI think I’m just… less engaged than I used to be. Friends will ask what’s going on, and I’ll give pretty short answers because explaining it feels like more effort than it should. I’ve skipped jazz nights or pickup basketball sometimes, and I don’t feel as bad about missing them as I probably should.\n\nWith family, I still talk to them, but I’m probably flatter, or less responsive. I don’t mean to be rude. It’s more like I can still get things done in pieces, but I don’t have the same spark for keeping up with people. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your work performance or ability to carry out daily tasks since these feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I don’t want to overstate it, because I am still working and I’m not, you know, unable to function.\n\nBut my practice is definitely smaller than it was. I’ve missed some follow-ups that I probably would’ve stayed on top of before, and I avoid certain public panels or events if I think it might put too much attention on who I’m meeting with. I’m also just not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to.\n\nSo… yes, it’s affected things, but I’m still managing day to day. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the signals you think are being sent to you through invitations, panel topics, or seating arrangements as unusual or hard to understand for most people?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, probably. I mean, I can see how if I explained it to someone else, they might say it sounds like I’m reading too much into normal things.\n\nBut to me, it doesn’t feel random. Like an invitation will come through, or a panel topic will be just close enough to something I’ve been dealing with, or I’ll notice who ends up sitting near me at an event, and it feels like there’s a message in it—like people in those arts and finance circles are sort of keeping track of who I’m meeting with.\n\nI know that may sound unusual. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I don’t want to sound unreasonable, but… I have a hard time letting it go once I notice it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you describe the messages you think you're receiving through these signals as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. I’m aware of that.

It’s just that, from my side, it feels like there’s a pattern. Like certain people in the arts and finance circles around Cincinnati are quietly keeping tabs on which clients I’m meeting with, and then these little things happen—an invitation, a panel topic, who ends up sitting near me—and it feels like a signal.

I know saying it out loud can make it sound… maybe more unusual than I’d like. And I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence. But once I notice it, I can’t really let it go. Does that make sense?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… thanks. I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling that certain people around the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are… sort of keeping tabs on me. Like, which clients I meet with, where I show up.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little strange, and I’m not trying to make it into a big dramatic thing. It’s just… invitations I get, topics on panels, even where people sit near me at events — it starts to feel like they’re sending little signals. I do tell myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go, and it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these feelings of being kept tabs on?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about two months, give or take. It wasn’t like one clear day it started, more that I began noticing patterns with client meetings and those little signals, and then I realized I’d been thinking about it pretty consistently. I know two months maybe isn’t that long, but it’s been enough that I haven’t been able to just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the beliefs you have about people keeping tabs on you as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can see how most people would probably find it unusual, or maybe hard to follow if I explained all of it.\n\nI’m not trying to sound paranoid or anything. It’s just that I’ve gotten pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly watching which clients I meet with, and then sending these little signals — like through invitations, panel topics, or even who ends up sitting near me at events. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation… but I haven’t really been able to let it go.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that these people are actually keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: I’m… fairly certain, I guess. I don’t want to say I know it like a fact, because I realize that probably sounds unreasonable, and I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence.\n\nBut honestly, a lot of the time I feel convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I meet with and then sort of signaling things through invitations, panel topics, or even who sits near me. I know that may not sound very solid from the outside, but it’s hard for me to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever question or doubt whether these people are actually keeping tabs on you, or do you feel completely sure about it?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes, yeah. I mean, I’m not completely sure in the way I’d be sure about something I could prove.\n\nI’ll tell myself it could just be coincidence — like maybe the invitations or panel topics or who sits near me at events don’t mean anything. And I know that’s probably what you’d expect me to consider. But then when it keeps lining up with certain clients I’ve met with, I end up feeling pretty convinced again that people in those arts and finance circles are tracking it somehow.\n\nSo… I guess I have doubts, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being kept tabs on affected your daily routine or decisions in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m still working, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I guess my practice has gotten smaller than it used to be. I’ve missed some follow-ups with clients, and I avoid certain public panels or events if I feel like there might be too much attention on who I’m talking to. And I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage.\n\nI don’t know if that makes sense, or if I’m overstating it, but… it has changed how I do things day to day somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being kept tabs on affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A bit, maybe. Not in a dramatic way—I’m not cutting people off or anything.\n\nI think I’m just… less engaged than I used to be. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Objectively… yes, I can admit it sounds unusual. I’m not trying to say it would make sense to everyone.

It’s just hard because when I’m in the middle of it, it doesn’t feel strange—it feels like there are these little signals, like through invitations or panel topics or even where people sit near me, and it feels connected to which clients I’ve been meeting with.

I do tell myself, “Maybe this is coincidence,” and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation. But I can’t quite drop it once I notice it. I hope that doesn’t sound too extreme—I’m trying to be honest about it.

I know that may not sound very solid from the outside, but it’s hard for me to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever question or doubt whether these people are actually keeping tabs on you, or do you feel completely sure about it?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes, yeah. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… there’s definitely a possibility. I mean, I don’t want to come across like I’m insisting I’m right about it.

I do tell myself it could just be coincidence—like an invitation happens to line up with something I’ve been thinking about, or a panel topic just feels relevant, or someone sits near me and I read into it too much. I can see how that would be the ordinary explanation.

I know two months maybe isn’t that long, but it’s been enough that I haven’t been able to just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the beliefs you have about people keeping tabs on you as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can see how most people would probably find it unusual, or maybe hard to follow if I explained all of it.\n\nI’m not trying to sound paranoid or anything. I know that may not sound very solid from the outside, but it’s hard for me to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever question or doubt whether these people are actually keeping tabs on you, or do you feel completely sure about it?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes, yeah. I’m aware of that.\n\nIt’s just that, from my side, it feels like there’s a pattern. Like certain people in the arts and finance circles around Cincinnati are quietly keeping tabs on which clients I’m meeting with, and then these little things happen—an invitation, a panel topic, who ends up sitting near me—and it feels like a signal.\n\nI know saying it out loud can make it sound… maybe more unusual than I’d like. And I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence. But once I notice it, I can’t really let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say the ideas you have about people keeping tabs on you are strange or unusual, even to you, when you think about them objectively?\n\nPatient: Objectively… yes, I can admit it sounds unusual. I’m not trying to say it would make sense to everyone.\n\nIt’s just hard because when I’m in the middle of it, it doesn’t feel strange—it feels like there are these little signals, like through invitations or panel topics or even where people sit near me, and it feels connected to which clients I’ve been meeting with.\n\nI do tell myself, “Maybe this is coincidence,” and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation. But I can’t quite drop it once I notice it. I hope that doesn’t sound too extreme—I’m trying to be honest about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that these signals you're interpreting could have a more ordinary explanation that doesn't involve people keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: Yes… there’s definitely a possibility. I mean, I don’t want to come across like I’m insisting I’m right about it.\n\nI do tell myself it could just be coincidence—like an invitation happens to line up with something I’ve been thinking about, or a panel topic just feels relevant, or someone sits near me and I read into it too much. I can see how that would be the ordinary explanation.\n\nBut I guess the reason I came in is that even when I tell myself that, I can’t really let it go. It still feels like certain people in those arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking who I’m meeting with and sending little signals. I know that probably sounds like a lot… I’m just trying to explain it accurately.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow often do you find yourself completely convinced that people are keeping tabs on you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not all the time… I mean, I try not to be completely convinced, if that makes sense. I do remind myself it could be coincidence.

But honestly, when it happens—like with an invitation, or a panel topic, or seeing who sits near me at an event—it can feel pretty convincing in the moment. Like there’s a connection to which clients I’ve been meeting with, and certain people in those Cincinnati arts and finance circles are aware of it somehow.

I don’t want to sound unreasonable. I know I may be reading into things. But it’s been happening enough that I can’t really just shake it off, and that’s part of why I came in.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… thanks. I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling that certain people around the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are… sort of keeping tabs on me. Like, which clients I meet with, where I show up.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little strange, and I’m not trying to make it into a big dramatic thing. It’s just… invitations I get, topics on panels, even where people sit near me at events — it starts to feel like they’re sending little signals. I do tell myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go, and it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these feelings of being kept tabs on?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about two months, give or take. It wasn’t like one clear day it started, more that I began noticing patterns with client meetings and those little signals, and then I realized I’d been thinking about it pretty consistently. I know two months maybe isn’t that long, but it’s been enough that I haven’t been able to just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the beliefs you have about people keeping tabs on you as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can see how most people would probably find it unusual, or maybe hard to follow if I explained all of it.\n\nI’m not trying to sound paranoid or anything. It’s just that I’ve gotten pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly watching which clients I meet with, and then sending these little signals — like through invitations, panel topics, or even who ends up sitting near me at events. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation… but I haven’t really been able to let it go.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that these people are actually keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: I’m… fairly certain, I guess. I don’t want to say I know it like a fact, because I realize that probably sounds unreasonable, and I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence.\n\nBut honestly, a lot of the time I feel convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I meet with and then sort of signaling things through invitations, panel topics, or even who sits near me. I know that may not sound very solid from the outside, but it’s hard for me to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever question or doubt whether these people are actually keeping tabs on you, or do you feel completely sure about it?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes, yeah. I mean, I’m not completely sure in the way I’d be sure about something I could prove.\n\nI’ll tell myself it could just be coincidence — like maybe the invitations or panel topics or who sits near me at events don’t mean anything. And I know that’s probably what you’d expect me to consider. But then when it keeps lining up with certain clients I’ve met with, I end up feeling pretty convinced again that people in those arts and finance circles are tracking it somehow.\n\nSo… I guess I have doubts, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being kept tabs on affected your daily routine or decisions in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m still working, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I guess my practice has gotten smaller than it used to be. I’ve missed some follow-ups with clients, and I avoid certain public panels or events if I feel like there might be too much attention on who I’m talking to. And I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage.\n\nI don’t know if that makes sense, or if I’m overstating it, but… it has changed how I do things day to day somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being kept tabs on affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A bit, maybe. Not in a dramatic way—I’m not cutting people off or anything.\n\nI think I’m just… less engaged than I used to be. Friends will ask what’s going on, and I’ll give pretty short answers because explaining it feels like more effort than it should. I’ve skipped jazz nights or pickup basketball sometimes, and I don’t feel as bad about missing them as I probably should.\n\nWith family, I still talk to them, but I’m probably flatter, or less responsive. I don’t mean to be rude. It’s more like I can still get things done in pieces, but I don’t have the same spark for keeping up with people. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your work performance or ability to carry out daily tasks since these feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I don’t want to overstate it, because I am still working and I’m not, you know, unable to function.\n\nBut my practice is definitely smaller than it was. I’ve missed some follow-ups that I probably would’ve stayed on top of before, and I avoid certain public panels or events if I think it might put too much attention on who I’m meeting with. I’m also just not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to.\n\nSo… yes, it’s affected things, but I’m still managing day to day. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the signals you think are being sent to you through invitations, panel topics, or seating arrangements as unusual or hard to understand for most people?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, probably. I mean, I can see how if I explained it to someone else, they might say it sounds like I’m reading too much into normal things.\n\nBut to me, it doesn’t feel random. Like an invitation will come through, or a panel topic will be just close enough to something I’ve been dealing with, or I’ll notice who ends up sitting near me at an event, and it feels like there’s a message in it—like people in those arts and finance circles are sort of keeping track of who I’m meeting with.\n\nI know that may sound unusual. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I don’t want to sound unreasonable, but… I have a hard time letting it go once I notice it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the messages you think you're receiving through these signals as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. I’m aware of that.\n\nIt’s just that, from my side, it feels like there’s a pattern. Like certain people in the arts and finance circles around Cincinnati are quietly keeping tabs on which clients I’m meeting with, and then these little things happen—an invitation, a panel topic, who ends up sitting near me—and it feels like a signal.\n\nI know saying it out loud can make it sound… maybe more unusual than I’d like. And I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence. But once I notice it, I can’t really let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say the ideas you have about people keeping tabs on you are strange or unusual, even to you, when you think about them objectively?\n\nPatient: Objectively… yes, I can admit it sounds unusual. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think most people would probably hear the details and think they’re strange, or at least a stretch.

I mean, it’s not like I’m saying there’s some obvious written message. It’s more that I notice things like invitations, or the topic of a panel, or who ends up sitting near me at events, and it feels like it lines up with which clients I’ve been meeting with. And then I start thinking certain people in the Cincinnati arts and finance circles are aware of it somehow.

I know that probably sounds unrealistic when I say it out loud. I do tell myself it could just be coincidence. I just… I have trouble letting it go once I’ve noticed the pattern. I hope that answers what you’re asking.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… thanks. I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling that certain people around the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are… sort of keeping tabs on me. Like, which clients I meet with, where I show up.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little strange, and I’m not trying to make it into a big dramatic thing. It’s just… invitations I get, topics on panels, even where people sit near me at events — it starts to feel like they’re sending little signals. I do tell myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go, and it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these feelings of being kept tabs on?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about two months, give or take. It wasn’t like one clear day it started, more that I began noticing patterns with client meetings and those little signals, and then I realized I’d been thinking about it pretty consistently. I know two months maybe isn’t that long, but it’s been enough that I haven’t been able to just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the beliefs you have about people keeping tabs on you as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can see how most people would probably find it unusual, or maybe hard to follow if I explained all of it.\n\nI’m not trying to sound paranoid or anything. It’s just that I’ve gotten pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly watching which clients I meet with, and then sending these little signals — like through invitations, panel topics, or even who ends up sitting near me at events. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation… but I haven’t really been able to let it go.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that these people are actually keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: I’m… fairly certain, I guess. I don’t want to say I know it like a fact, because I realize that probably sounds unreasonable, and I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence.\n\nBut honestly, a lot of the time I feel convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I meet with and then sort of signaling things through invitations, panel topics, or even who sits near me. I know that may not sound very solid from the outside, but it’s hard for me to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever question or doubt whether these people are actually keeping tabs on you, or do you feel completely sure about it?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes, yeah. I mean, I’m not completely sure in the way I’d be sure about something I could prove.\n\nI’ll tell myself it could just be coincidence — like maybe the invitations or panel topics or who sits near me at events don’t mean anything. And I know that’s probably what you’d expect me to consider. But then when it keeps lining up with certain clients I’ve met with, I end up feeling pretty convinced again that people in those arts and finance circles are tracking it somehow.\n\nSo… I guess I have doubts, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being kept tabs on affected your daily routine or decisions in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m still working, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I guess my practice has gotten smaller than it used to be. I’ve missed some follow-ups with clients, and I avoid certain public panels or events if I feel like there might be too much attention on who I’m talking to. And I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage.\n\nI don’t know if that makes sense, or if I’m overstating it, but… it has changed how I do things day to day somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being kept tabs on affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A bit, maybe. Not in a dramatic way—I’m not cutting people off or anything.\n\nI think I’m just… less engaged than I used to be. Friends will ask what’s going on, and I’ll give pretty short answers because explaining it feels like more effort than it should. I’ve skipped jazz nights or pickup basketball sometimes, and I don’t feel as bad about missing them as I probably should.\n\nWith family, I still talk to them, but I’m probably flatter, or less responsive. I don’t mean to be rude. It’s more like I can still get things done in pieces, but I don’t have the same spark for keeping up with people. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your work performance or ability to carry out daily tasks since these feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I don’t want to overstate it, because I am still working and I’m not, you know, unable to function.\n\nBut my practice is definitely smaller than it was. I’ve missed some follow-ups that I probably would’ve stayed on top of before, and I avoid certain public panels or events if I think it might put too much attention on who I’m meeting with. I’m also just not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to.\n\nSo… yes, it’s affected things, but I’m still managing day to day. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the signals you think are being sent to you through invitations, panel topics, or seating arrangements as unusual or hard to understand for most people?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, probably. I mean, I can see how if I explained it to someone else, they might say it sounds like I’m reading too much into normal things.\n\nBut to me, it doesn’t feel random. Like an invitation will come through, or a panel topic will be just close enough to something I’ve been dealing with, or I’ll notice who ends up sitting near me at an event, and it feels like there’s a message in it—like people in those arts and finance circles are sort of keeping track of who I’m meeting with.\n\nI know that may sound unusual. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I don’t want to sound unreasonable, but… I have a hard time letting it go once I notice it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the messages you think you're receiving through these signals as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. I’m aware of that.\n\nIt’s just that, from my side, it feels like there’s a pattern. Like certain people in the arts and finance circles around Cincinnati are quietly keeping tabs on which clients I’m meeting with, and then these little things happen—an invitation, a panel topic, who ends up sitting near me—and it feels like a signal.\n\nI know saying it out loud can make it sound… maybe more unusual than I’d like. And I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence. But once I notice it, I can’t really let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say the ideas you have about people keeping tabs on you are strange or unusual, even to you, when you think about them objectively?\n\nPatient: Objectively… yes, I can admit it sounds unusual. I’m not trying to say it would make sense to everyone.\n\nIt’s just hard because when I’m in the middle of it, it doesn’t feel strange—it feels like there are these little signals, like through invitations or panel topics or even where people sit near me, and it feels connected to which clients I’ve been meeting with.\n\nI do tell myself, “Maybe this is coincidence,” and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation. But I can’t quite drop it once I notice it. I hope that doesn’t sound too extreme—I’m trying to be honest about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that these signals you're interpreting could have a more ordinary explanation that doesn't involve people keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: Yes… there’s definitely a possibility. I mean, I don’t want to come across like I’m insisting I’m right about it.\n\nI do tell myself it could just be coincidence—like an invitation happens to line up with something I’ve been thinking about, or a panel topic just feels relevant, or someone sits near me and I read into it too much. I can see how that would be the ordinary explanation.\n\nBut I guess the reason I came in is that even when I tell myself that, I can’t really let it go. It still feels like certain people in those arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking who I’m meeting with and sending little signals. I know that probably sounds like a lot… I’m just trying to explain it accurately.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself completely convinced that people are keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: Not all the time… I mean, I try not to be completely convinced, if that makes sense. I do remind myself it could be coincidence.\n\nBut honestly, when it happens—like with an invitation, or a panel topic, or seeing who sits near me at an event—it can feel pretty convincing in the moment. Like there’s a connection to which clients I’ve been meeting with, and certain people in those Cincinnati arts and finance circles are aware of it somehow.\n\nI don’t want to sound unreasonable. I know I may be reading into things. It still feels like certain people in those arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking who I’m meeting with and sending little signals. I know that probably sounds like a lot… I’m just trying to explain it accurately.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself completely convinced that people are keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: Not all the time… I mean, I try not to be completely convinced, if that makes sense. I do remind myself it could be coincidence.\n\nBut honestly, when it happens—like with an invitation, or a panel topic, or seeing who sits near me at an event—it can feel pretty convincing in the moment. It varies… I don’t want to overstate it. It’s not like I’m sitting there every minute thinking about it.

But on days when something happens—like I get an invitation, or I see a panel topic that feels connected, or someone ends up sitting near me at an event—I can spend a lot of time turning it over in my head. I know two months maybe isn’t that long, but it’s been enough that I haven’t been able to just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the beliefs you have about people keeping tabs on you as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can see how most people would probably find it unusual, or maybe hard to follow if I explained all of it.\n\nI’m not trying to sound paranoid or anything. It’s just that I’ve gotten pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly watching which clients I meet with, and then sending these little signals — like through invitations, panel topics, or even who ends up sitting near me at events. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation… but I haven’t really been able to let it go.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that these people are actually keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: I’m… fairly certain, I guess. I don’t want to say I know it like a fact, because I realize that probably sounds unreasonable, and I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence.\n\nBut honestly, a lot of the time I feel convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I meet with and then sort of signaling things through invitations, panel topics, or even who sits near me. I know that may not sound very solid from the outside, but it’s hard for me to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever question or doubt whether these people are actually keeping tabs on you, or do you feel completely sure about it?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes, yeah. I mean, I’m not completely sure in the way I’d be sure about something I could prove.\n\nI’ll tell myself it could just be coincidence — like maybe the invitations or panel topics or who sits near me at events don’t mean anything. And I know that’s probably what you’d expect me to consider. But then when it keeps lining up with certain clients I’ve met with, I end up feeling pretty convinced again that people in those arts and finance circles are tracking it somehow.\n\nSo… I guess I have doubts, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being kept tabs on affected your daily routine or decisions in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m still working, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I guess my practice has gotten smaller than it used to be. I’ve missed some follow-ups with clients, and I avoid certain public panels or events if I feel like there might be too much attention on who I’m talking to. And I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage.\n\nI don’t know if that makes sense, or if I’m overstating it, but… it has changed how I do things day to day somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being kept tabs on affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A bit, maybe. Not in a dramatic way—I’m not cutting people off or anything.\n\nI think I’m just… less engaged than I used to be. Friends will ask what’s going on, and I’ll give pretty short answers because explaining it feels like more effort than it should. I’ve skipped jazz nights or pickup basketball sometimes, and I don’t feel as bad about missing them as I probably should.\n\nWith family, I still talk to them, but I’m probably flatter, or less responsive. I don’t mean to be rude. It’s more like I can still get things done in pieces, but I don’t have the same spark for keeping up with people. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your work performance or ability to carry out daily tasks since these feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I don’t want to overstate it, because I am still working and I’m not, you know, unable to function.\n\nBut my practice is definitely smaller than it was. I’ve missed some follow-ups that I probably would’ve stayed on top of before, and I avoid certain public panels or events if I think it might put too much attention on who I’m meeting with. I’m also just not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to.\n\nSo… yes, it’s affected things, but I’m still managing day to day. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the signals you think are being sent to you through invitations, panel topics, or seating arrangements as unusual or hard to understand for most people?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, probably. I mean, I can see how if I explained it to someone else, they might say it sounds like I’m reading too much into normal things.\n\nBut to me, it doesn’t feel random. Like an invitation will come through, or a panel topic will be just close enough to something I’ve been dealing with, or I’ll notice who ends up sitting near me at an event, and it feels like there’s a message in it—like people in those arts and finance circles are sort of keeping track of who I’m meeting with.\n\nI know that may sound unusual. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I don’t want to sound unreasonable, but… I have a hard time letting it go once I notice it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the messages you think you're receiving through these signals as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. I’m aware of that.\n\nIt’s just that, from my side, it feels like there’s a pattern. Like certain people in the arts and finance circles around Cincinnati are quietly keeping tabs on which clients I’m meeting with, and then these little things happen—an invitation, a panel topic, who ends up sitting near me—and it feels like a signal.\n\nI know saying it out loud can make it sound… maybe more unusual than I’d like. And I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence. But once I notice it, I can’t really let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say the ideas you have about people keeping tabs on you are strange or unusual, even to you, when you think about them objectively?\n\nPatient: Objectively… yes, I can admit it sounds unusual. I’m not trying to say it would make sense to everyone.\n\nIt’s just hard because when I’m in the middle of it, it doesn’t feel strange—it feels like there are these little signals, like through invitations or panel topics or even where people sit near me, and it feels connected to which clients I’ve been meeting with.\n\nI do tell myself, “Maybe this is coincidence,” and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation. But I can’t quite drop it once I notice it. I hope that doesn’t sound too extreme—I’m trying to be honest about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that these signals you're interpreting could have a more ordinary explanation that doesn't involve people keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: Yes… there’s definitely a possibility. I mean, I don’t want to come across like I’m insisting I’m right about it.\n\nI do tell myself it could just be coincidence—like an invitation happens to line up with something I’ve been thinking about, or a panel topic just feels relevant, or someone sits near me and I read into it too much. I can see how that would be the ordinary explanation.\n\nBut I guess the reason I came in is that even when I tell myself that, I can’t really let it go. It still feels like certain people in those arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking who I’m meeting with and sending little signals. I know that probably sounds like a lot… I’m just trying to explain it accurately.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself completely convinced that people are keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: Not all the time… I mean, I try not to be completely convinced, if that makes sense. I do remind myself it could be coincidence.\n\nBut honestly, when it happens—like with an invitation, or a panel topic, or seeing who sits near me at an event—it can feel pretty convincing in the moment. Like there’s a connection to which clients I’ve been meeting with, and certain people in those Cincinnati arts and finance circles are aware of it somehow.\n\nI don’t want to sound unreasonable. I know I may be reading into things. But it’s been happening enough that I can’t really just shake it off, and that’s part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the specific details of the messages you think you're receiving as strange or unrealistic to most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably hear the details and think they’re strange, or at least a stretch.\n\nI mean, it’s not like I’m saying there’s some obvious written message. It’s more that I notice things like invitations, or the topic of a panel, or who ends up sitting near me at events, and it feels like it lines up with which clients I’ve been meeting with. And then I start thinking certain people in the Cincinnati arts and finance circles are aware of it somehow.\n\nI know that probably sounds unrealistic when I say it out loud. I do tell myself it could just be coincidence. I just… I have trouble letting it go once I’ve noticed the pattern. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: How much time would you say you spend each day thinking about the idea that people are keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: It varies… I don’t want to overstate it. It’s not like I’m sitting there every minute thinking about it.\n\nBut on days when something happens—like I get an invitation, or I see a panel topic that feels connected, or someone ends up sitting near me at an event—I can spend a lot of time turning it over in my head. Maybe an hour here and there, sometimes more, kind of checking it against which clients I’ve met with and whether it really means anything.\n\nEven when I tell myself it could just be coincidence, it’s hard for me to fully drop it. I’ll go back to it later in the day. I know that probably sounds… not ideal. But that’s honestly why I came in, because I can’t really let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not trying to say it would make sense to everyone.\n\nIt’s just hard because when I’m in the middle of it, it doesn’t feel strange—it feels like there are these little signals, like through invitations or panel topics or even where people sit near me, and it feels connected to which clients I’ve been meeting with.\n\nI do tell myself, “Maybe this is coincidence,” and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation. But I can’t quite drop it once I notice it. I hope that doesn’t sound too extreme—I’m trying to be honest about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that these signals you're interpreting could have a more ordinary explanation that doesn't involve people keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: Yes… there’s definitely a possibility. I mean, I don’t want to come across like I’m insisting I’m right about it.\n\nI do tell myself it could just be coincidence—like an invitation happens to line up with something I’ve been thinking about, or a panel topic just feels relevant, or someone sits near me and I read into it too much. I can see how that would be the ordinary explanation.\n\nBut I guess the reason I came in is that even when I tell myself that, I can’t really let it go. It still feels like certain people in those arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking who I’m meeting with and sending little signals. It’s more than occasional, I think… though I wouldn’t say it’s constant every second.

It’s kind of a background presence most days. I came in because for a while now I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in the Cincinnati arts and finance circles are quietly tracking which clients I meet with, and then I notice these little things—like invitations, panel topics, or who sits near me at events—and it feels like they’re signals.

I do try to talk myself down and say, “Maybe this is just coincidence,” because I know that’s probably what most people would say. But once it’s in my head, I can’t really let it go. So… more than just popping in now and then, I guess. Does that answer it okay?

I know that may not sound very solid from the outside, but it’s hard for me to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever question or doubt whether these people are actually keeping tabs on you, or do you feel completely sure about it?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes, yeah. Not in a dramatic way—I’m not cutting people off or anything.\n\nI think I’m just… less engaged than I used to be. Friends will ask what’s going on, and I’ll give pretty short answers because explaining it feels like more effort than it should. I’ve skipped jazz nights or pickup basketball sometimes, and I don’t feel as bad about missing them as I probably should.\n\nWith family, I still talk to them, but I’m probably flatter, or less responsive. I don’t mean to be rude. It’s more like I can still get things done in pieces, but I don’t have the same spark for keeping up with people. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your work performance or ability to carry out daily tasks since these feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I don’t want to overstate it, because I am still working and I’m not, you know, unable to function.\n\nBut my practice is definitely smaller than it was. I’ve missed some follow-ups that I probably would’ve stayed on top of before, and I avoid certain public panels or events if I think it might put too much attention on who I’m meeting with. I’m also just not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to.\n\nSo… yes, it’s affected things, but I’m still managing day to day. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the signals you think are being sent to you through invitations, panel topics, or seating arrangements as unusual or hard to understand for most people?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, probably. I mean, I can see how if I explained it to someone else, they might say it sounds like I’m reading too much into normal things.\n\nBut to me, it doesn’t feel random. Like an invitation will come through, or a panel topic will be just close enough to something I’ve been dealing with, or I’ll notice who ends up sitting near me at an event, and it feels like there’s a message in it—like people in those arts and finance circles are sort of keeping track of who I’m meeting with.\n\nI know that may sound unusual. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I don’t want to sound unreasonable, but… I have a hard time letting it go once I notice it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the messages you think you're receiving through these signals as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. I’m aware of that.\n\nIt’s just that, from my side, it feels like there’s a pattern. Like certain people in the arts and finance circles around Cincinnati are quietly keeping tabs on which clients I’m meeting with, and then these little things happen—an invitation, a panel topic, who ends up sitting near me—and it feels like a signal.\n\nI know saying it out loud can make it sound… maybe more unusual than I’d like. And I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence. But once I notice it, I can’t really let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say the ideas you have about people keeping tabs on you are strange or unusual, even to you, when you think about them objectively?\n\nPatient: Objectively… yes, I can admit it sounds unusual. I’m not trying to say it would make sense to everyone.\n\nIt’s just hard because when I’m in the middle of it, it doesn’t feel strange—it feels like there are these little signals, like through invitations or panel topics or even where people sit near me, and it feels connected to which clients I’ve been meeting with.\n\nI do tell myself, “Maybe this is coincidence,” and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation. But I can’t quite drop it once I notice it. I hope that doesn’t sound too extreme—I’m trying to be honest about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that these signals you're interpreting could have a more ordinary explanation that doesn't involve people keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: Yes… there’s definitely a possibility. I mean, I don’t want to come across like I’m insisting I’m right about it.\n\nI do tell myself it could just be coincidence—like an invitation happens to line up with something I’ve been thinking about, or a panel topic just feels relevant, or someone sits near me and I read into it too much. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s distressing enough that I came in, though. For a while now I’ve had this feeling that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with, and then sending little signals through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events.\n\nI do tell myself it could just be coincidence, and I know that’s probably the reasonable answer. But once I notice it, I can’t really let it go, and that’s what’s been upsetting. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that thinking about people keeping tabs on you is something that pops into your head occasionally or is it more of a constant presence in your daily thoughts?\n\nPatient: It’s more than occasional, I think… though I wouldn’t say it’s constant every second.\n\nIt’s kind of a background presence most days. I came in because for a while now I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in the Cincinnati arts and finance circles are quietly tracking which clients I meet with, and then I notice these little things—like invitations, panel topics, or who sits near me at events—and it feels like they’re signals.\n\nI do try to talk myself down and say, “Maybe this is just coincidence,” because I know that’s probably what most people would say. But once it’s in my head, I can’t really let it go. So… more than just popping in now and then, I guess. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s because I did anything wrong, exactly. At least… I hope not.

It feels more like it’s tied to my work and who I meet with—like certain people in the arts and finance circles here notice which clients I’m connected to, and then these little things happen afterward. Invitations, panel topics, someone sitting near me at an event… it starts to feel like they’re signaling that they know.

I realize that may sound like I’m reading too much into it. I do tell myself it could be coincidence. But once I’ve made that connection in my head, it’s hard for me to just drop it. Does that make sense?

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… thanks. I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling that certain people around the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are… sort of keeping tabs on me. Like, which clients I meet with, where I show up.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little strange, and I’m not trying to make it into a big dramatic thing. It’s just… invitations I get, topics on panels, even where people sit near me at events — it starts to feel like they’re sending little signals. I do tell myself it could just be coincidence, but I can’t really let it go, and it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these feelings of being kept tabs on?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about two months, give or take. It wasn’t like one clear day it started, more that I began noticing patterns with client meetings and those little signals, and then I realized I’d been thinking about it pretty consistently. I know two months maybe isn’t that long, but it’s been enough that I haven’t been able to just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the beliefs you have about people keeping tabs on you as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can see how most people would probably find it unusual, or maybe hard to follow if I explained all of it.\n\nI’m not trying to sound paranoid or anything. It’s just that I’ve gotten pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly watching which clients I meet with, and then sending these little signals — like through invitations, panel topics, or even who ends up sitting near me at events. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation… but I haven’t really been able to let it go.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that these people are actually keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: I’m… fairly certain, I guess. I don’t want to say I know it like a fact, because I realize that probably sounds unreasonable, and I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence.\n\nBut honestly, a lot of the time I feel convinced that certain people in those arts and finance circles are tracking who I meet with and then sort of signaling things through invitations, panel topics, or even who sits near me. I know that may not sound very solid from the outside, but it’s hard for me to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever question or doubt whether these people are actually keeping tabs on you, or do you feel completely sure about it?\n\nPatient: I do question it sometimes, yeah. I mean, I’m not completely sure in the way I’d be sure about something I could prove.\n\nI’ll tell myself it could just be coincidence — like maybe the invitations or panel topics or who sits near me at events don’t mean anything. And I know that’s probably what you’d expect me to consider. But then when it keeps lining up with certain clients I’ve met with, I end up feeling pretty convinced again that people in those arts and finance circles are tracking it somehow.\n\nSo… I guess I have doubts, but I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being kept tabs on affected your daily routine or decisions in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m still working, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I guess my practice has gotten smaller than it used to be. I’ve missed some follow-ups with clients, and I avoid certain public panels or events if I feel like there might be too much attention on who I’m talking to. And I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage.\n\nI don’t know if that makes sense, or if I’m overstating it, but… it has changed how I do things day to day somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of being kept tabs on affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A bit, maybe. Not in a dramatic way—I’m not cutting people off or anything.\n\nI think I’m just… less engaged than I used to be. Friends will ask what’s going on, and I’ll give pretty short answers because explaining it feels like more effort than it should. I’ve skipped jazz nights or pickup basketball sometimes, and I don’t feel as bad about missing them as I probably should.\n\nWith family, I still talk to them, but I’m probably flatter, or less responsive. I don’t mean to be rude. It’s more like I can still get things done in pieces, but I don’t have the same spark for keeping up with people. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your work performance or ability to carry out daily tasks since these feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I don’t want to overstate it, because I am still working and I’m not, you know, unable to function.\n\nBut my practice is definitely smaller than it was. I’ve missed some follow-ups that I probably would’ve stayed on top of before, and I avoid certain public panels or events if I think it might put too much attention on who I’m meeting with. I’m also just not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to.\n\nSo… yes, it’s affected things, but I’m still managing day to day. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the signals you think are being sent to you through invitations, panel topics, or seating arrangements as unusual or hard to understand for most people?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, probably. I mean, I can see how if I explained it to someone else, they might say it sounds like I’m reading too much into normal things.\n\nBut to me, it doesn’t feel random. Like an invitation will come through, or a panel topic will be just close enough to something I’ve been dealing with, or I’ll notice who ends up sitting near me at an event, and it feels like there’s a message in it—like people in those arts and finance circles are sort of keeping track of who I’m meeting with.\n\nI know that may sound unusual. I do tell myself it could be coincidence, and I don’t want to sound unreasonable, but… I have a hard time letting it go once I notice it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the messages you think you're receiving through these signals as something that most people would find hard to understand or unusual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably find it hard to understand. I’m aware of that.\n\nIt’s just that, from my side, it feels like there’s a pattern. Like certain people in the arts and finance circles around Cincinnati are quietly keeping tabs on which clients I’m meeting with, and then these little things happen—an invitation, a panel topic, who ends up sitting near me—and it feels like a signal.\n\nI know saying it out loud can make it sound… maybe more unusual than I’d like. And I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence. But once I notice it, I can’t really let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say the ideas you have about people keeping tabs on you are strange or unusual, even to you, when you think about them objectively?\n\nPatient: Objectively… yes, I can admit it sounds unusual. I’m not trying to say it would make sense to everyone.\n\nIt’s just hard because when I’m in the middle of it, it doesn’t feel strange—it feels like there are these little signals, like through invitations or panel topics or even where people sit near me, and it feels connected to which clients I’ve been meeting with.\n\nI do tell myself, “Maybe this is coincidence,” and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation. But I can’t quite drop it once I notice it. I hope that doesn’t sound too extreme—I’m trying to be honest about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that these signals you're interpreting could have a more ordinary explanation that doesn't involve people keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: Yes… there’s definitely a possibility. I mean, I don’t want to come across like I’m insisting I’m right about it.\n\nI do tell myself it could just be coincidence—like an invitation happens to line up with something I’ve been thinking about, or a panel topic just feels relevant, or someone sits near me and I read into it too much. I can see how that would be the ordinary explanation.\n\nBut I guess the reason I came in is that even when I tell myself that, I can’t really let it go. It still feels like certain people in those arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking who I’m meeting with and sending little signals. I know that probably sounds like a lot… I’m just trying to explain it accurately.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself completely convinced that people are keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: Not all the time… I mean, I try not to be completely convinced, if that makes sense. I do remind myself it could be coincidence.\n\nBut honestly, when it happens—like with an invitation, or a panel topic, or seeing who sits near me at an event—it can feel pretty convincing in the moment. Like there’s a connection to which clients I’ve been meeting with, and certain people in those Cincinnati arts and finance circles are aware of it somehow.\n\nI don’t want to sound unreasonable. I know I may be reading into things. But it’s been happening enough that I can’t really just shake it off, and that’s part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the specific details of the messages you think you're receiving as strange or unrealistic to most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably hear the details and think they’re strange, or at least a stretch.\n\nI mean, it’s not like I’m saying there’s some obvious written message. It’s more that I notice things like invitations, or the topic of a panel, or who ends up sitting near me at events, and it feels like it lines up with which clients I’ve been meeting with. And then I start thinking certain people in the Cincinnati arts and finance circles are aware of it somehow.\n\nI know that probably sounds unrealistic when I say it out loud. I do tell myself it could just be coincidence. I just… I have trouble letting it go once I’ve noticed the pattern. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: How much time would you say you spend each day thinking about the idea that people are keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: It varies… I don’t want to overstate it. It’s not like I’m sitting there every minute thinking about it.\n\nBut on days when something happens—like I get an invitation, or I see a panel topic that feels connected, or someone ends up sitting near me at an event—I can spend a lot of time turning it over in my head. Maybe an hour here and there, sometimes more, kind of checking it against which clients I’ve met with and whether it really means anything.\n\nEven when I tell myself it could just be coincidence, it’s hard for me to fully drop it. I’ll go back to it later in the day. I know that probably sounds… not ideal. But that’s honestly why I came in, because I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: How much distress are these thoughts causing you right now?\n\nPatient: Right now… I’d say it’s bothering me, but I’m still functioning. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s distressing enough that I came in, though. For a while now I’ve had this feeling that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with, and then sending little signals through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events.\n\nI do tell myself it could just be coincidence, and I know that’s probably the reasonable answer. But once I notice it, I can’t really let it go, and that’s what’s been upsetting. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that thinking about people keeping tabs on you is something that pops into your head occasionally or is it more of a constant presence in your daily thoughts?\n\nPatient: It’s more than occasional, I think… though I wouldn’t say it’s constant every second.\n\nIt’s kind of a background presence most days. And I do try to tell myself it could just be coincidence. But once I notice it, I can’t really let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say the ideas you have about people keeping tabs on you are strange or unusual, even to you, when you think about them objectively?\n\nPatient: Objectively… yes, I can admit it sounds unusual. I’m not trying to say it would make sense to everyone.\n\nIt’s just hard because when I’m in the middle of it, it doesn’t feel strange—it feels like there are these little signals, like through invitations or panel topics or even where people sit near me, and it feels connected to which clients I’ve been meeting with.\n\nI do tell myself, “Maybe this is coincidence,” and I know that’s probably the more reasonable explanation. But I can’t quite drop it once I notice it. I hope that doesn’t sound too extreme—I’m trying to be honest about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that these signals you're interpreting could have a more ordinary explanation that doesn't involve people keeping tabs on you?\n\nPatient: Yes… there’s definitely a possibility. I mean, I don’t want to come across like I’m insisting I’m right about it.\n\nI do tell myself it could just be coincidence—like an invitation happens to line up with something I’ve been thinking about, or a panel topic just feels relevant, or someone sits near me and I read into it too much. I can see how that would be the ordinary explanation.\n\nBut I guess the reason I came in is that even when I tell myself that, I can’t really let it go. It still feels like certain people in those arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking who I’m meeting with and sending little signals. I mean… I suppose it could be false. I know that’s the answer that probably makes the most sense from the outside.

But it doesn’t feel false when it’s happening.
